| **[HarnessRL: Co-Evolving Policies with a Just-in-Time Training Harness for Open-Ended RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26426v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Rubric-based reinforcement learning (RL) trains policies for open-ended generation with natural-language criteria as rewards, but these rewards can become ineffective as training progresses. HarnessRL maintains a training-time memory from which a harness evolver retrieves within-task history and cross-task evidence. Using this evidence, the evolver attributes each reward failure to the interface whose update can address it. It then runs a proposer and just-in-time critic loop in which the critic accepts a candidate update only after measuring its effect on the current frozen policy, returning rejected candidates for revision. | **[ForagerSim: Evaluating Embodied Map-Based Reasoning in Large-Scale Dynamic Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26395v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Animals build cognitive maps of vast environments, use them to perform map-based navigation, and might even be reusing the same circuitry used in spatial reasoning for creating and using maps of arbitrary task-spaces. In contrast, current embodied AI systems either rely on hand-engineered mapping pipelines, which break in the presence of unmodeled dynamics and are restricted to metric space, or on learned policies that rarely form map-like representations at all. Developing architectures that could learn to build and use map-like representations as flexibly as animals is, however, difficult because few embodied AI benchmarks actually require map-based reasoning. To address this, we present ForagerSim --- an interdisciplinary research platform and simulation environment focused on embodied tasks that require map-based reasoning, with scenarios ranging from mobile-robot search and rescue to animal foraging in both complex mazes and vast, potentially infinite open areas. ForagerSim is highly modular and allows modifying independent axes of difficulty related to perception (abstract 2D sensing to full RGB or LiDAR), scale (room-scale to multi-km scale), topology (open-fields to complex mazes), dynamics (smoke, debris, sensorimotor failures and others) and tasks (from simple 2D coverage to foraging-inspired well-alternation tasks). We compare reinforcement learning baselines with a simple engineered robotics stack and a human player on 8 example worlds which show how different difficulty axes affect existing methods. Our results show that, when sensing is simplified to 2D, learned policies can perform multi-object search and well-alternation tasks surprisingly well in simple topologies, but they fall short of human performance in larger or more complex-topology environments where map-based baselines perform well, suggesting that map-based reasoning is the bottleneck in these scenarios.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code and data: https://github.com/MrTomzor/foragersim</p></details> |

Moreover, ActiveMem enables compact open-weight models to achieve competitive performance with substantially larger proprietary systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[CodeSkill: Latent Skill Abstraction for Long-Horizon Code Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33243v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Code agents require long-horizon decision-making over complex interaction trajectories. However, existing reinforcement learning (RL) approaches typically optimize behavior at the token level, creating a mismatch between low-level generation and high-level behavioral reasoning. This limitation leads to inefficient exploration and weak credit assignment under sparse rewards. Moreover, while large-scale agent trajectories often contain recurring multi-step behavioral patterns, their noisy token-level representations hinder effective experience reuse. To address these challenges, we propose CodeSkill, a framework that adapts hierarchical latent skill modeling to the code agent domain. CodeSkill first leverages a teacher model to distill both successful and failed trajectories into multi-level textual abstractions. It then integrates temporal variational inference with reinforcement learning to map these discrete semantics into continuous latent variables, while an adaptive boundary mechanism dynamically gates skill transitions based on execution feedback. The learned skills are injected into a frozen LLM policy as latent semantic prefixes, enabling optimization in a compact semantic space rather than over raw token sequences. By shifting RL from token-level exploration to experience-level reasoning, CodeSkill improves optimization efficiency and long-horizon behavioral coherence. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CodeSkill achieves highly competitive performance against strong open-weight baselines across diverse general and industrial coding benchmarks. | **[Mobile Embodied Agent Network (MEAN): Integrated Framework, Enabling Technologies, and Task-Driven Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26297v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mobile networks are evolving from human-centered, data-oriented communication infrastructures toward machine- and/or agent-dominant, task-oriented platforms that are to serve the rapidly increasing networked machines and embodied agents. In order to fulfill the newly arose complex task objectives, the framework of future mobile network is to be innovated to reflect and facilitate the intrinsic interplay and integration of the underlying processes of perception, communication, computation, decision, action, and feedback, etc. This survey establishes the mobile embodied agent network (MEAN) as a task-oriented networking paradigm in which networking and distributed intelligence are jointly organized around closed-loop embodied task execution. We first establish a unified MEAN framework that explains how network resources and intelligent functions jointly support embodied task execution, and extend its performance indicators from conventional communication metrics to embodied task outcomes and cross-layer system effects. With this framework, we analyze three complementary enabling mechanisms that form a cognition-interaction-adaption loop. Large artificial intelligence (AI) models provide high-level cognition, token communication connects this cognition with distributed agents through shared semantic representations, and reinforcement learning closes the loop by adapting system operation to network, task, and physical feedback. We further investigate the bidirectional interplay between MEAN and emerging technologies, showing how they mutually extend system capabilities and reshape task-oriented network design. | **[Toward Online Robust Zero-Sum Markov Games with Function Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26286v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Online robust zero-sum Markov games pose a coupled operator-learning and game-solving problem: from nominal interaction data, the learner must recover a worst-case minimax Bellman operator while maintaining strategic coverage and controlling stage-game approximation error. We develop an optimistic dual robust Bellman framework that combines dual robust Bellman fitting, optimistic minimax planning, backward-validated confidence sets, and exploiter-mix data collection. Our analysis is organized around the robust Bellman residuals induced by the candidate value class and yields a regret reduction that separates exploration complexity, robust operator-estimation error, stage-game solution error, and misspecification. Under explicit interpretable assumptions, we obtain conditional theorem interfaces for general and projected function approximation, together with closed results in several structured regimes, including tabular, epochwise cross-fit linear, self-normalized pure-online linear / finite-rank kernel, and projected RKHS truncation. | **[RLTLDR: Self-improvement by Internalizing Self-generated Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37633v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The common paradigm of reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is to let agents make multiple attempts at a task, and optimize towards the successful ones. In this paper, we introduce RLTLDR. After each failed attempt, we show the policy the verifier outputs and let it write its own feedback, in the form of a single TL;DR insight. The next rollout is conditioned on all previous insights, and we sequentially sample rollouts until a solution is found. Moreover, we enable backpropagation on the in-context insights to internalize a direct task to insight mapping. On challenging tool-calling and coding datasets (filtered to Pass@128=0), standard GRPO training of a Qwen 3.5 9B Thinking policy stays flat at a Pass@1 of 0% to 1%. RLTLDR breaks through this learning barrier, achieving a Pass@1 of 14-31% with insights in context during training and, crucially, 12-13% when no insight is in context at eval time. We identify that the key is the task to insight internalization. To study this further, we reduce our approach to SFTLDR, training only on (task, insight) tuples, without showing or backpropagating on any rollouts. Training on only 4k of these tuples recovers almost the full performance of RLTLDR and classical SFT on full rollouts. This demonstrates a promising compacted training paradigm of the form "on this sort of task, keep this sort of thing in mind", which we hope to inspire future research on.</p></details> |  |
| **[Humanoid Badminton: Learning Dynamic Racket Skills from Limited Human Motion Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31840v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High-speed racket sports provide a demanding testbed for humanoid robots, requiring time-critical decisions, precise striking, and dynamic whole-body coordination. Direct tracking provides insufficient executable variation for diverse shuttle conditions, while purely task-driven optimization may produce unnatural motion. We therefore present a three-stage hierarchical reinforcement learning framework for dynamic humanoid badminton. First, task-randomized motion augmentation expands sparse annotated hitting events into executable target-conditioned stroke variations, forming a continuous latent skill space. Second, a high-level planner outputs continuous latent skill codes to compose these skills online according to the observed shuttle state. Third, a context-conditioned adversarial regularizer encourages more natural planner-level skill usage while preserving return performance. In real-world deployment, our system sustains multi-skill rallies with human players, including forehand, backhand, and highly dynamic jump returns. | **[Reinforcement Learning Controllers for Fusion Scenario Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26138v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scenario development, the design of the waveforms that take a tokamak discharge from breakdown to flat-top, is still an iterative, labour-intensive process. So the process is iterative and includes a number of trial-and-error steps. We propose to replace the per-scenario controller tuning by a single goal-conditioned reinforcement-learning controller trained once, in advance, against a free-boundary equilibrium and transport simulator. A scenario is then specified only by the target waveforms of the physical quantities of interest (plasma current, line-averaged electron density, LCFS shape, x-point positions), and the controller produces the coil-current and fueling commands that realize them. We demonstrate the approach on an EAST plasma-current ramp-up: the controller takes over a small limited plasma, ramps the current and the density, grows the cross-section, forms two x-points and holds a double-null diverted configuration against sampled shape targets for the rest of a 1.2 s episode. The neural network controller is trained using TQC algorithm. On 1024 test scenarios sampled from a low-discrepancy scrambled Sobol sequence in 11-dimensional target space, the controller forms the divertor in 86% of the cases, holds it for more than 0.5 s in 67% and reaches the end of the episode in a diverted state in 58%, with flat-top tracking errors of a few kA in plasma current, $\sim 0.15\times10^{19}$ m$^{-3}$ in density, $\sim 2$ cm in position and $\sim 8$ cm in x-point location; the failures concentrate at the high-current density ($J$) region which is believed to be physically or from engineering perspective not feasible within ohmic mode.</p></details> |  |

| **[Distilling LLM Feedback for Lean Theorem Proving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30861v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Post-training for reasoning models typically combines supervised fine-tuning with reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards, most commonly with GRPO. | **[FrameGRPO: Incentivizing Video Reasoning via Multi-Frame Rollouts and Temporal Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26022v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, particularly Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), has improved video reasoning in multimodal large language models. | **[Learning to Reason Efficiently with Interventional Step Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25876v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large reasoning models often produce lengthy chain-of-thought (CoT) traces mixing useful intermediate steps with unnecessary ones. Despite their effectiveness, such signals reflect the model's behavior at a step rather than that step's contribution to the answer. To address this, we propose the interventional step credit (ISC), which takes a causal-intervention view and assesses step importance to the reasoning through targeted parameter-level interventions. Building on ISC, we further propose IGPO, which uses ISC to guide fine-grained advantage rescaling in reinforcement learning, reducing redundant steps while preserving the important ones. Experiments across two model scales and nine benchmarks show that IGPO achieves up to 62% average token reduction and better overall performance among compared methods.</p></details> | 15 pages |
| **[CODA: Cohort- and Drift-aware Foundation Model for Multimodal Clinical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25871v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in multimodal foundation models have demonstrated strong performance across diverse clinical tasks. However, existing approaches predominantly operate at the individual sample level and fail to model the interplay between population-level cohort patterns and patient-specific variation that underlie real-world clinical reasoning. In practice, clinicians routinely reason through patient cohorts sharing similar demographic, physiological, or pathological characteristics, while accounting for patient-specific deviations from the group prototype. Motivated by this gap, we propose CODA, a cohort- and drift-aware foundation model for multimodal clinical reasoning. CODA explicitly integrates cohort structure into the modeling pipeline through two complementary mechanisms: cohort-aware population stratification and drift-aware individual adaptation. In particular, we leverage reinforcement learning to discover clinically meaningful patient cohorts, and further model each patient's deviation from cohort prototypes as a structured drift signal encoding fine-grained individual heterogeneity. These cohort- and drift-aware representations are incorporated into the foundation model to enable reasoning that is simultaneously informed by population-level patterns and sensitive to individual-level variation. Extensive experiments on real-world electronic health record (EHR) benchmarks demonstrate that CODA achieves consistent effectiveness and strong generalizability across diverse clinical tasks, including closed-QA, open-QA, and report generation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Adaptive Decoding Methods for Token-Efficient Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25860v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Rollout decoding affects both which trajectories are used for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) and how many completion tokens are spent collecting them. | **[Rubrics Beyond Evaluation: Exploiting Rubric Structure in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25850v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Rubrics are widely used to evaluate open-ended question answering by breaking overall quality into explicit criteria. This leaves their capability structure largely unused in guiding the training process. At its core, RSRL builds on three key designs: (1) Rubric-to-Capability Structuring first recovers the capability structure hidden in rubric criteria, (2) Self-Sufficient Capability Learning then uses this structure to bootstrap underdeveloped capabilities into effective learning, (3) Capability-Residual Credit Routing finally preserves capability-specific gains during optimization. Experiments show that RSRL consistently improves both overall performance and underdeveloped capabilities, boosting downstream scientific question-answering performance by up to 7.43 over the initial policy and 7.60 over non-rubric structured RL baselines, showing that reinforcement learning can substantially benefit from the capability structure encoded in rubrics.</p></details> |  |

| **[Where RL Breaks: Localizing and Repairing Failures via Targeted Continued Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25836v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is widely used to improve reasoning in language models, yet growing evidence suggests that it mostly sharpens what the base model can already do. We look inside the RL policy's own rollouts and find that failures are localized: whether a rollout fails is strongly predicted by a small set of characteristic n-grams, ranging from reflection phrases such as "let's recheck" to specific operations such as "the inverse of A". These failures persist throughout RL training, although they are not beyond the model's reach: other sampled answers to the same question are often correct. Building on this observation, we use the failure $n$-grams to retrieve text from the pretraining corpus and continue pretraining the base model on it before RL. The resulting corpus has only 35M tokens, 3--4 orders of magnitude smaller than typical mid-training. We evaluate this approach on OLMo2 and Qwen3 models trained with RL on (1) GSM8K; (2) a mixture of math, commonsense, and knowledge questions; and (3) general math and science reasoning. Despite its size, the corpus yields consistent gains: our approach outperforms continued pretraining on the same amount of text sampled from the pretraining corpus across 10 out of 11 settings, including by $2.0$ points on GSM8K when inserted before mid-training. The retrieved text also transfers across model families: text retrieved for OLMo2-1B's failures improves Qwen3-4B on the mixture by $1.9$ points over the same baseline. (2026) showed that in low-data reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, training GRPO on mixed-difficulty prompts gives up to 5x the sample efficiency of easy-only prompts. We test this on their procedural counting task with Qwen3-4B-Base and LoRA, comparing GRPO to rejection-sampling fine-tuning (RFT) on the same prompts, verifier and token cap. We use three seeds per arm. Hypotheses and falsifiers were registered before training. To rule out a poorly tuned baseline, RFT receives a 27-run hyperparameter sweep, while GRPO uses Bauer et al.'s recipe unchanged. GRPO outperforms RFT at every prompt set: by 10.9 points on held-out test problems with curated prompts, and, in post hoc comparisons with matched rollout budgets, by 8.4 and 9.2 points on the easy and mixed prompts. | **[Long-Horizon Multi-Object Rearrangement via Distilled Tree Search over Continuous Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25785v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite recent advances in policy learning, long-horizon manipulation remains difficult because learned policies must avoid compounding errors while preserving future feasibility. To this end, we propose DISCO: Distilled Tree Search over Continuous Actions. DISCO uses progressive-widening MCTS in physics simulation to generate long-horizon plans. During search, a learned policy proposes continuous actions, while a learned value function scores actions by their expected progress toward task completion. Using GPU-parallelized simulation, DISCO periodically distills MCTS-generated plans into the policy-value model, improving the action proposals and value estimates used in subsequent searches. Finally, the learned policy-value model can be directly deployed without the need for simulator-based search at test time. Across simulated and real-world rearrangement tasks, DISCO outperforms behavior cloning, reinforcement learning, and search-based baselines.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at CoRL 2026 (Oral). Project page: https://distilled-search.github.io</p></details> |

| **[Remember Less, Adapt Longer: Bounded-State In-Context Reinforcement Learning with Recurrent Summary Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25758v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In-context reinforcement learning (ICRL) enables agents to adapt to new tasks without parameter updates, relying on memory to retain what they learn from experience. For agents operating continually, existing approaches using memory trade off bounded cost against access to past experience. Fixed-size memories bound storage but restrict access: sliding windows discard older steps, while recurrent networks compress even recent interactions into a hidden state. We introduce Recurrent Summary Memory (RSM), a Transformer policy that attends exactly to a short working buffer of recent interactions and carries a fixed-capacity summary of earlier experience. When the buffer fills, RSM rewrites the summary from its previous contents and the buffer, then clears the buffer, so deployment memory and per-decision cost stay $O(1)$ in task length. On long-running partially observable tasks, frozen RSM policies sustain their adapted performance for eight times the training horizon with about 1% of full-history memory, ending ahead of every evaluated baseline while growing memories deteriorate. When the task configuration changes mid-run, RSM re-adapts better than growing and recurrent memories. Clearing the summary or blocking the policy's access to it removes its benefit, while swapping in another task's summary steers behaviour towards that task. Together, these results show that our fixed-capacity summary can sustain in-context adaptation far beyond the training horizon.</p></details> |  |
| **[MAPS: A Multi-Agent Personalized Script-Driven Video Advertising System Enhanced by Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25748v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With the rapid growth of online video consumption, video advertising has become a dominant component of digital advertising. To address this limitation, we propose a multi-agent personalized script-driven video advertising system (MAPS). We design four collaborative agents that form a closed-loop pipeline of user profile modeling, script generation, video synthesis, and feedback-driven iterative refinement. To train the Scripter Agent, we develop a reinforcement learning framework equipped with a multi-dimensional reward function, which steers the agent toward producing scripts that simultaneously align with user content preferences and target product information. Extensive offline experiments on representative datasets demonstrate that our approach achieves encouraging performance in generating and integrating personalized creatives. Online A/B experiments in a specific scenario in the Kuaishou advertising system show that MAPS improves CTR by 2.87%, CVR by 2.74%, and ARPU by 9.85% over the generative baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[Anatomy of Off-Policy Policy Gradient: Importance Sampling, KL Regularization, and Baselines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25725v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning has become a central tool for large language model (LLM) post-training, where policy gradient methods are routinely deployed off-policy, even though vanilla policy gradient assumes on-policy sampling. Focusing on the KL route, we identify it with the group-relative policy gradient (GRPG) update, establish an equivalence to a trajectory-level Bellman residual minimization objective, and obtain a finite-sample regret bound under all-policy coverage. This also gives a theoretical account of a variant of the empirically successful GRPO objective. We further uncover a hidden role of baselines in the off-policy setting: a constant shift in the baseline implements pessimism, the standard remedy in offline RL for partial data coverage. We instantiate this via the asymmetric REINFORCE objective, and connect it to the game-theoretic offline RL framework, showing a regret bound with single-policy coverage. Together, these results offer a unified theoretical view of off-policy policy gradient in LLM post-training and connect it to classical ideas in offline RL.</p></details> |  |

| **[DeReCo: Decoupling Representation and Coordination Learning for Object-Adaptive Decentralized Multi-Robot Cooperative Grasping and Lifting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08111v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generalizing decentralized multi-robot cooperative grasping and lifting across objects with diverse shapes and physical properties remains a fundamental challenge. A typical approach jointly optimizes object-dependent representations and coordinated policies in an end-to-end manner while randomizing object shapes and physical properties during training. Inspired by prior work on decoupling representation and control learning in single-robot settings, we propose DeReCo, a MARL framework that decouples representation and coordination learning for object-adaptive decentralized multi-robot cooperative grasping and lifting. DeReCo adopts a two-phase training strategy: (1) coordination learning with privileged object information, and (2) transition to decentralized execution through reconstruction of object-dependent representations from local observations and retraining of coordination policies using the reconstructed representations. | **[ToolOPD: Credit-Aware On-Policy Distillation for Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25693v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>On-policy distillation (OPD) enhances the capabilities of small language agents by providing dense, token-level supervision from a teacher model on student-generated trajectories. However, standard OPD implicitly assumes that credit is uniformly distributed across long-horizon trajectories, assigning the same strength of teacher supervision to different actions. Through credit-distribution analysis and outcome-reversal counterfactual experiments, we find that trajectory credit is concentrated on a small number of critical actions. Moreover, even when the trajectory is held fixed, reversing only the final outcome substantially changes the credit assigned to multiple interaction turns. These findings show that the uniform-credit assumption fails to identify critical decisions and ultimately degrades the model's reasoning accuracy. To address these challenges, we propose ToolOPD, an outcome-aware on-policy distillation framework designed for long-horizon tool use. ToolOPD first summarizes each completed trajectory and its outcome into a reusable hindsight skill. It then measures how this skill changes the teacher's probability of each student action token and converts the change into outcome-aware credit. The resulting credit preserves supervision that supports successful decisions or discourages failed ones, while suppressing irrelevant and conflicting signals. ToolOPD uses these credits to reweight the action-level distillation loss and jointly optimizes it with reinforcement learning. As a result, AUVs primarily rely on passive observation, which yields incomplete local perception and limited task efficiency. However, both are insufficient to characterize the actual contribution of perceptual information to cooperative tasks under realistic covert physical communication conditions. This paper proposes a Sensed-information Value Realization Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (SVR-MARL) framework that leverages practical information to characterize information utility for cooperative tasks and learns distributed cooperative policies under realistic communication and covert constraints. Through a case study of covert multi-AUV cooperative localization and tracking, the potential of the proposed framework to improve collaborative task efficiency while reducing unnecessary communication and exposure risks is demonstrated.</p></details> |  |

| **[ProCredit: From Outcome Rewards to Progress Credit in Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27532v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon agentic tasks require an agent to modify an environment through a sequence of tool calls, with success determined by the final state. The standard recipe assigns a single outcome reward at the end and compares trajectories sampled for the same task. As a result, a group with no successful trajectory yields no training signal, failed attempts cannot be told apart by how close they came to completion, and turns that advance the task receive the same credit as turns that only query the environment. | **[Think, Compress, Answer: Reinforcement Learning for Multimodal Grounding via Reasoning Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25640v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extended reasoning has substantially improved multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on tasks requiring compositional and relational inference. | **[Trackable Skill Library Lifecycle Management for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25126v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Skill-augmented agentic RL has emerged as an effective approach to improving LLM agents on long-horizon tasks. Despite this progress, individual skills may follow distinct trajectories as the policy evolves, with some becoming redundant after the model internalizes their capabilities, others remaining valuable as external scaffolding, and still others turning harmful. However, existing skill library systems fail to identify each skill's true contribution due to free-riding among co-retrieved skills, difficulty confounding across tasks, and the absence of skill-specific counterfactual evidence, which prevents reliable skill lifecycle management. We propose TrackRL, an agentic RL framework where skill tracking drives skill library full lifecycle management. Specifically, within each GRPO group, we turn grouped sampling into online randomized controlled experiments. Independent random injection and within-group contrasts on the same task isolate each skill's true contribution, and reliability-weighted aggregation accumulates evidence across groups. As evidence accumulates, we continuously track skill utility, which measures the skill's causal contribution as external scaffolding, and model mastery, which reflects how far the corresponding capability has been internalized. Along this line, we formulate skill lifecycle management as an evidence-gated state-transition process that governs each skill from generation and admission through protected incubation to retention, retirement, or removal, while a soft capacity bound controls library growth. Experiments show that TrackRL outperforms recent skill-augmented RL baselines in both library-assisted and skill-free evaluation, with further analyses revealing how skill value shifts during policy learning and how evidence-driven state transitions shape skill library evolution. Our code is available at https://github.com/xizhu1022/TrackRL.</p></details> |  |
| **[ClinSDT: LLM-Encoded Clinical Semantic Guidance for Sepsis Treatment via Decision Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25121v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Offline sepsis treatment aims to learn treatment policies from retrospective ICU trajectories. Decision Transformer-based methods provide a natural sequence modeling framework for this task, but they mainly rely on raw physiological states and historical clinician actions. Such raw trajectory tokens may not explicitly capture clinical semantics that clinicians use in practice, such as patient severity, recent treatment response, and current risk priorities. To address this limitation, we propose ClinSDT, an LLM-assisted Decision Transformer framework for offline sepsis treatment. ClinSDT uses a frozen LLM as a semantic bridge rather than a treatment decision maker. It constructs three clinical semantic views from ICU trajectories, namely Profile, Context, and Advisory, and inserts their encoded representations as independent tokens into a Decision Transformer policy. To support policy refinement and rollout, ClinSDT further trains a Treatment-Response Forecaster to estimate the next physiological state, SAPS-II change, and predictive uncertainty from state-action inputs. In the second training stage, the forecaster screens local counterfactual treatment candidates, and only low-uncertainty candidates with lower predicted SAPS-II increase are distilled into the policy after semantic regeneration. During inference, ClinSDT performs closed-loop treatment rollout by refreshing semantic inputs from the currently available patient state. Experiments on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV show that ClinSDT achieves consistent relative improvements over representative imitation learning, offline reinforcement learning, and Decision Transformer baselines under the same rollout protocol. Code and data are available at https://github.com/KAG778/ClinSDT.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 6 figures. Accepted at CIKM 2026 (Full Research Track, Oral Presentation)</p></details> |
| **[Self-organizing reinforcement learning: a swarm approaches the explore-exploit optimum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25113v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The explore/exploit trade-off is the core problem of reinforcement learning (RL). We show that a minimal foraging swarm approaches its optimum - the ideal-free / Nash allocation of a multi-agent resource-sharing game (an equilibrium, not a return maximum) - with no learner and no reward signal. Agents explore by heavy-tailed (Levy) flights, recruit neighbours to a discovery through a threshold cascade on their proximity network, and disperse as a patch crowds; this cascade cycle balances exploration and exploitation and drives foragers toward the ideal free distribution. The swarm attains the shape of this optimum (committed foragers track richness with slope 0.94) but not its exact equilibrium - it stays measurably exploitable - reaching it dynamically (~150 steps) through the coupling of recruitment to movement: remove the coupling and matching collapses (0.94 -> 0.08). The RL sits at the level of the problem and the allocation reached, not the micro-rule: no step evaluates payoff (reward-free by construction, though the crowd signal is informationally tied to payoff), and the swarm reaches the allocation without learning it (quality plateaus and re-settles after resources move, no cumulative gain). Against a trained baseline the comparison is two-sided: in an easier, disembodied game a learner is more precise, while in the same embodied environment the reward-free swarm reaches ~65% of a validated oracle ceiling at zero training and leads until trained PPO and centralised-critic (CTDE) learners overtake it (~74-75%, 16 seeds, all above the swarm mean) after ~4,000 of 15,000 training steps - its advantage is in budget, not asymptote. In this one setting we read it not as beating RL but as a strong zero-shot, reward-free operating point: a demonstration that the self-organizing physics of a minimal swarm can carry a collective close to the optimum RL seeks, with none of RL's machinery.</p></details> | <details><summary>16 pa...</summary><p>16 pages, 9 figures, code and data: https://github.com/galenwilkerson/self-organizing-reinforcement-learning</p></details> |
| **[Explore or Confirm? LLM Agents Misallocate Measurements Under Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25111v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As model reasoning gets cheaper, the physical experiment becomes the slow and costly step in discovery. Each experiment returns a noisy reading: a high value can mean a good candidate or a lucky draw. With a fixed budget, an AI agent must keep choosing between exploring a new candidate and confirming a promising one by re-testing it. We measure that choice in Wellocate, a simulated assay campaign with known true values. It is built on a generated landscape and on two real protein datasets, GB1 and PhoQ, scrambled so no model can answer from memory. We test five language models as minimal agents, with no tools and no memory beyond the history in each prompt. They misallocate in both directions. Three over-confirm, spending most of the budget re-testing. On the generated landscape one under-confirms, trusting single readings, and there every model ends with a worse final pick than a basic scripted strategy. Each failure has its own cause. The over-confirmers run short of new candidates worth trying: a list of untried candidates cuts their re-testing and, on real protein data, makes two of them pick much better. A running summary of their readings or a description of the landscape changes it by at most five percentage points. For the under-confirmer, re-testing its top candidates in a final round, replayed on its own campaigns, recovers only about a quarter of what it loses by picking the wrong candidate. This suggests confirmation has to run through the whole campaign. We release Wellocate as a benchmark and reinforcement-learning environment with frozen splits and scripted baselines. With it, anyone can measure how an agent splits its budget before trusting it with real experiments, and train agents to split it better. Code, data and the environment are available at https://github.com/Xstendyl/wellocate.</p></details> | <details><summary>38 pa...</summary><p>38 pages, 12 figures. Code, data and environment: https://github.com/Xstendyl/wellocate</p></details> |
| **[EVA: Aligning Video World Models with Executable Robot Actions via Inverse Dynamics Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17808v3)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Video generative models offer a promising basis for robotic planning by predicting future visual rollouts from observations and task instructions. However, their training objectives typically lack explicit constraints on robot executability. Generated rollouts can exhibit embodiment-related artifacts, including arm deformation and temporal discontinuities, while the actions decoded from these rollouts may be unstable or violate robot motion limits. We term this mismatch between generated robot behavior and embodiment-specific execution requirements the executability gap. We introduce Executable Video Alignment (EVA), a post-training framework that uses action-space feedback to improve both the visual integrity of generated robot motion and the feasibility of decoded actions. EVA trains an inverse dynamics model (IDM) on robot demonstrations and freezes it to decode actions from generated videos. We compute a reward from these actions that penalizes non-smooth motion and violations of velocity and acceleration limits, and use it to guide reinforcement-learning updates to the video generator. By feeding decoded-action constraints back into video generation, EVA links visual and action-level improvements through a shared alignment signal. Experiments on RoboTwin, LIBERO, and a real bimanual robot show that EVA reduces embodiment-related visual artifacts and improves task success when executing IDM-decoded actions. Further experiments show that video models fine-tuned with EVA generate more effective video--action pairs for training downstream VLAs.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://eva-project-page.github.io/</p></details> |

| **[Physical Words: A Vocabulary for Structuring Latent Reasoning in Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26854v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal reasoning provides robotic foundation models with predictive context needed for reliable action generation. However, language-based reasoning captures task semantics but struggles to express fine-grained physical dynamics, while latent reasoning models future state evolution but offers limited structure for organizing these dynamics into reusable reasoning primitives. To bridge this gap, we introduce PhysWord, a reasoning-before-acting foundation model built on Physical Words, which use atomic action verbs as shared semantic anchors for continuous representations of physical dynamics. Specifically, PhysWord predicts upcoming atomic action words and directly aligns their contextual hidden states with latent targets derived from the corresponding future observations. Building on these primitives, we construct a temporally structured PhysWord space with visual and optional tactile variants of each action word. Autoregressively predicting Physical Words across future timesteps captures transitions between execution stages, providing structured multimodal context for action generation. PhysWord achieves state-of-the-art success rates of 90.3\% on visuotactile UniVTAC and 99.6\% on vision-based LIBERO-Long, and outperforms competing methods on real-world tasks using bimanual grippers and dexterous hands. Further evaluations demonstrate that our structured latent reasoning framework generalizes to unseen scenarios and enables long-horizon manipulation.</p></details> |  |
| **[CoopUQ: Robust Multi-Robot Social Navigation via Uncertainty-Aware Cooperative Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26852v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning enables mobile robots to make fast and scalable navigation decisions in constrained human-shared environments. However, directly extending single-robot approaches to multi-robot settings often fails to ensure safety and efficiency, especially under occlusions and out-of-distribution (OOD) scenarios, where individual robots may overlook potential risks or make inaccurate predictions. Leveraging cooperation effectively for both prediction and navigation remains a major challenge. We propose CoopUQ, a multi-robot social navigation framework that bridges uncertainty-aware cooperative occupancy forecasting and robust decision making. CoopUQ predicts future occupancy grid maps through cross-robot spatiotemporal feature fusion and further leverages forecasts and their uncertainty to guide policy via constrained multi-agent reinforcement learning, where the cost penalizes navigation through regions predicted to be occupied or highly uncertain. This design enables the policy to adapt its risk sensitivity under distribution shifts. Experimental results across multiple Unity3D-based simulation environments with both in-distribution and OOD settings, including diverse layouts, pedestrian densities, and rushing behavior, demonstrate improved prediction performance and safety over representative baselines. Real-robot experiments further demonstrate its robustness across various scenes. Our code and videos are available at https://coopuq.github.io/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Confe...</summary><p>Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL 2026); Project Website: https://coopuq.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[Sequential Planning via Anchored Robotic Keypoints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30613v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Changes in a robot's observations do not automatically imply changes in the task it is solving, but language-conditioned manipulation systems often fail to separate the two. If an object moves, for example, the locations at which actions should be executed may change while the underlying manipulation plan remains valid. We introduce Sequential Planning via Anchored Robotic Keypoints (SPARK), which separates a task's symbolic plan from its execution-time perceptual grounding, and we experimentally isolate whether a fixed symbolic plan can survive substantial changes to the scene through execution-time re-grounding. SPARK represents the task as a symbolic behavior tree whose object references are grounded to current 3D observations during execution, allowing the robot to update where actions are performed while preserving the plan. Runtime verification then enables local recovery and renewed grounding, only resorting to a new plan if necessary. To test the separation between plan and perceptual-grounding, we perform a set of manipulation experiments with a fixed plan while moving objects during execution. Under a 10 cm displacement, verification and adaptation maintain $66.7\%$ task success, compared with $2.2\%$ when disabled, demonstrating the importance of the underlying principle. Without task-specific model training and with an open-weight planner, SPARK also achieves $66.8\%$ mean success across six LIBERO-PRO settings, and $68\%$ across eleven physical task-embodiment settings on three robot platforms. Together, these results show that robustness does not require re-planning the task and for an important class of failures, it is sufficient to preserve the plan and update the system's perception of a changing world. Code and videos can be found at \url{https://cwru-aism.github.io/spark-page/}</p></details> | 29 pages, 7 figures |
| **[Bio-inspired Thrust-Assisted Trotting on a legged-Aerial Robot via Real-Time QP-MPC](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26810v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents the hardware implementation of a quadratic program-based controller on Harpy: thruster-assisted bipedal robot to perform thruster-assisted trotting. This is inspired by wing-assisted locomotion observed in birds such as the chukar partridge, which employ thrust vectoring to manipulate the ground reaction force. The proposed controller formulates the thruster-assisted spring-loaded inverted pendulum dynamics as a condensed QP-based model predictive control problem, enabling simultaneous regulation of ground contact force and active thrust vectoring at 500 Hz. A decoupled control architecture is used, where the robot's sagittal dynamics are controlled using QP-MPC while the frontal dynamics is regulated using a cascade orientation stabilization controller. Two experiments were conducted with different forward reference velocities of 0 m/s and 0.1 m/s. While the instantaneous velocity oscillates, the mean velocities can track their respective reference. Additionally, joint tracking error remains within 1 deg while tracking the desired leg length given by the leg trajectory controller and desired swing trajectory given by Raibert's heuristic. The QP-MPC problem is consistently solved with 0.18ms, which is well within the 2ms computation budget while satisfying the GRF friction cone constraint.</p></details> | <details><summary>To be...</summary><p>To be published in IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS 2026)</p></details> |
| **[LLM-Flock: LLM-Driven Decentralized Multi-Robot Flocking via Influence-Based Consensus with Collision Avoidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06513v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong capabilities in reasoning and planning, prompting researchers to explore their use as decentralized decision-makers for multi-robot formation control. However, directly applying LLMs to such tasks often leads to unstable behavior, in which robots may collapse to the centroid of their positions or diverge entirely, due to hallucinated reasoning and limited coordination awareness. We propose LLM-Flock, a decentralized multi-robot flocking framework that addresses these challenges through two key mechanisms: (1) an influence-based consensus protocol, where robots iteratively refine individually generated formation plans by adopting those of more connected neighbors, driving the team toward a unified plan without centralized control, and (2) a two-layer collision avoidance strategy that pairs LLM-based urgency reasoning with a programmatic collision checker and re-prompting mechanism. We evaluate LLM-Flock across five state-of-the-art LLMs, including both closed-source (GPT-5, Claude 4.5) and open-source (Llama3.1-405b, Qwen-Max, DeepSeek-R1) models, demonstrating notable improvements in formation stability and convergence over prior LLM-based methods. | **[Robot Learning with Vision-Language-Action Models for Agricultural Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26717v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic manipulation in agricultural environments remains challenging due to variations in plant geometry, occlusion, and camera viewpoints. Pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) and generalist robot policies offer a promising approach to addressing these challenges by learning manipulation behaviors from multimodal observations and language instructions. However, adapting these policies to agricultural tasks requires domain-specific demonstrations, which are costly and time-consuming to collect on physical robots. In this work, we develop a simulation-to-real robot-learning framework for agricultural reaching and grasping that integrates task-specific demonstration collection and pretrained policy adaptation. A MuJoCo-based agricultural environment is developed with diverse tomato plant structures to support demonstration collection and controlled evaluation. Expert demonstrations are collected through joystick teleoperation, with a low-level controller used to execute robot actions. Two pretrained generalist robot policies, Octo and $π_{0.5}$, are adapted using simulated demonstrations, followed by further fine-tuning on a physical Franka Research 3 manipulator using real-robot demonstrations. The adapted policies are evaluated under in-distribution, viewpoint/spatial-shift, and out-of-distribution conditions in simulation, followed by physical-robot experiments. The influence of proprioceptive information on policy performance is also investigated. Results show that $π_{0.5}$ achieves higher task-performance scores than Octo and exhibits less degradation under distribution shifts. | **[VQActFlow: Vector-Quantized Action Mode Steering for Multi-Task Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21600v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-task robot manipulation policies are challenging to learn from demonstration because traditionally a single network must select among qualitatively different action modes from a multimodal demonstration distribution, conditioned on language and visual context. We propose VQActFlow, a multi-task manipulation policy that tokenizes action chunks and generates code sequences via Variational Flow Matching. VQActFlow maintains an explicit preference over action modes throughout generation. Inference-time guidance acts on this preference to steer mode commitment. We instantiate this with classifier-free guidance over language conditioning, which steers the policy toward the instructed action mode, and a learned codebook critic that supplies a complementary feasibility signal. We evaluate VQActFlow on three platforms: the LIBERO simulation benchmarks, a Unitree G1 humanoid performing whole-body pick-and-place, and an ALOHA-style bimanual platform performing contact-rich tasks. Across these benchmarks, VQActFlow outperforms both continuous and discrete baselines.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 7 figures, 5 tables; Accepted at IEEE RA-L, 2026; Project website https://vqactflow.github.io/vqactflow/</p></details> |
| **[Robotic Physical Comparison of Biologically Motivated and Counterfactual Sensory Arrangements using Biomimetic Skin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26680v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human skin contains diverse mechanoreceptors, including Merkel cells and Pacinian corpuscles, each localized in specific regions. In this study, we developed a layered biomimetic robotic skin designed to mimic key structural features of human skin tissue by embedding receptor-analog sensors within compliant soft media. Arrangements reflecting key features of the biologically motivated sensory organization exhibited higher performance than the alternative configurations examined. This study addresses a biological question concerning how spatial organization influences tactile function by presenting a biomimetic robotic approach that enables physical and empirical comparison of biologically motivated and counterfactual sensory arrangements that are difficult to investigate in living systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in Bioinspiration & Biomimetics (IOP Publishing). Project page: https://poyotamu000.github.io/biomimetic-skin-sensory-arrangements/</p></details> |
| **[RoboValue: A Fine-Grained Sim-and-Real Benchmark for Unified Evaluation of Robotic Value Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26663v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic value models assess task execution from visual observations and instructions, providing feedback for policy learning and execution monitoring. | **[A Probabilistic, Rotation-Preserving Kernel Operator Surrogate for Continuum-Robot Design Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26605v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We develop an uncertainty-aware, operator-learning-based surrogate for tendon-driven continuum robots (TDCRs) that generalizes across robot design and actuation while predicting the complete equilibrium configuration. | **[GROW$^2$: Grounding Which and Where for Robot Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30632v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Can the robot use a plate to cut a cake if no knife is available? | **[Learning Wheel Motion Uncertainty for Degeneracy-Aware LiDAR-Inertial Odometry on Quadrupedal Wheel-Legged Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26550v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Wheel kinematics can support odometry for quadrupedal wheel-legged robots when LiDAR geometry provides weak motion constraints. | **[RoboMMT: Learning Stable Tracking of Modulated Expressive and Interactive Robot Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26414v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Expressive robot motion can improve naturalness, effectiveness, and perceived likability in human-robot interaction. | **[RIFAR: Reliability-Informed and Forgetting-Aware Replay for Continual Robot Learning with World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03079v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Genuine embodied agency requires robots to turn continuous real-world experience into lasting, transferable skills. | **[Robotic Photography System based on Vision-Language-Action Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26287v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern robotic photography requires understanding photographic intent, planning camera motions, and producing aesthetically pleasing images in complex environments. We present RoboPhoto-VLA, a unified Vision-Language-Action framework that jointly models perception, reasoning, and camera control for autonomous robotic photography. RoboPhoto-VLA incorporates PhotoCoT for structured action planning and Aesthetic Direct Preference Optimization (ADPO) to align camera behaviors with human aesthetic preferences. To facilitate training and evaluation, we construct OpenPhotography, a robotic photography dataset with language-guided demonstrations. Extensive real-world experiments demonstrate that RoboPhoto-VLA produces photographs better aligned with human aesthetic preferences while generalizing effectively to unseen subjects, viewpoints, poses, and complex photographic instructions.</p></details> | VCIP2026 |
| **[PhantomTac: Hijacking Robotic Tactile Systems with Deploy-and-forget Tags](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26174v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tactile perception is crucial for precise robotic manipulation, providing an important foundation for dexterous, closed-loop force control. Among various tactile sensing techniques, magnetic tactile sensors stand out thanks to their fine-grained, three-dimensional force perception capability and strong robustness against visual occlusion. In this study, we discover a physical loophole called PhantomTac in magnetic tactile sensors. The attacker can alter the magnetic field, thus spoofing the force interpreted by magnetic tactile sensors. The attacker can stealthily deploy such an attack by simply attaching a thin (the thickness is around 0.5 mm), battery-free magnetic tag to the surface of an object. Hence, the attack tag can be easily concealed by a barcode sticker. Such an attack scheme is "deploy-and-forget" -- the attacker can deploy the tag and leave. When a robotic hand is picking up an object, it may severely damage the object. Specifically, spoofing the normal force can crush the object, whereas spoofing the shear force can lead to a loose grip, thus dropping the object. Compared with directly sabotaging the robotic hand, PhantomTac is much more concealed as the attacker does not need access to the device. We performed extensive reverse-engineering efforts to characterize the sensing mechanism of commercial-off-the-shelf tactile sensors and proposed a practical tag design. End-to-end evaluations conducted on real robots and robotic arms demonstrate that our attack can bypass existing anti-interference mechanisms and effectively hijack their closed-loop control processes, achieving an attack success rate exceeding 95% when the grasping-position offset is bounded within 8 mm. Finally, based on the limitations of existing anti-magnetic interference mechanisms, we propose a multimodal-consistency defense mechanism to mitigate such physical-layer security threats.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at ACM CCS 2026. Project page: https://phantomtac.org</p></details> |
| **[Data-Efficient Learning from Demonstration for Humanoid Robots via Feature-Level Perception and Prompt-Based Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26170v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Learning from Demonstration (LfD) allows robots to learn new skills from human examples; however, many vision-based methods require large datasets and resource-intensive end-to-end training. The proposed approach integrates layered visual preprocessing, Kinect-based human joint estimation, prompt-based object detection, and depth information to extract human motion features and three-dimensional object locations from demonstration videos. These features are used to train a temporal deep learning model that predicts robot joint-angle trajectories for execution. The framework is implemented on a NAO humanoid robot, evaluated quantitatively in simulation, and demonstrated on physical hardware. In simulation, the robot achieves a 78% success rate on a pick-and-place task using only 16 demonstrations, which increases to 86% when trained with 100 demonstrations. The framework also achieves a 71% success rate on a pushing task using eight demonstrations, highlighting its effectiveness for data-efficient humanoid skill acquisition.</p></details> | <details><summary>6 pag...</summary><p>6 pages, 4 figures, 2 tables. Presented at IEEE ISAIA 2026</p></details> |
| **[GemNav: Discrete-Token Visual Robot Navigation using a Multimodal Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06882v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual navigation policies built on large pretrained models increasingly rely on large cross-embodiment datasets and expressive action-generation mechanisms. We introduce GemNav, a visual navigation policy that represents each robot-local waypoint coordinate as a categorical distribution over discrete metric value bins and soft-decodes it into a continuous coordinate for execution. This probabilistic representation retains likelihood information over alternative coordinate values while providing a compact action space suitable for robot navigation. We further construct training trajectories using metric-distance goal sampling rather than fixed future frame offsets, exposing the policy to varying physical navigation horizons independent of dataset frame rate and robot speed. GemNav is trained on a single 8.7-hour public SCAND dataset, roughly three orders of magnitude smaller than competing cross-embodiment datasets. Extensive offline and closed-loop experiments show improved out-of-distribution generalization over frame-based sampling and substantially more reliable obstacle navigation than a matched continuous regression head. | **[A GPU-Resident Pipeline for Multi-Robot 3D Inspection with Limited Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26106v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present a complete, GPU-resident pipeline for multi-robot 3D inspection of underwater structures. Given a triangle mesh and a fleet of $K$ robots, each carrying a short-range, limited field-of-view camera, the pipeline computes 6-DOF trajectories that jointly cover a target fraction of the surface while minimizing a linearly interpolated makespan/total-cost objective. The joint problem is NP-hard, so we decompose it into five core stages - candidate viewpoint generation, visibility computation, set cover, min-max vehicle routing solved as a mixed integer program (MIP) with lifted Miller-Tucker-Zemlin subtour elimination, and priority-based multi-agent path finding with continuous-time inter-robot conflict checking - flanked by mesh loading and surface sampling as preprocessing and by trajectory smoothing as postprocessing. Our contributions are: an end-to-end GPU-accelerated integration of the five stages, with its kernel design documented; a GPU batch adaptation of Lien's $\varepsilon$-visibility with four custom CUDA kernels; a $β$-aware per-vehicle tour upper bound derived from the interpolated objective, supplementing the Desrochers-Laporte coefficient lift with forbidden-pair cuts; a 4D space-time extension of Zhou and Zeng's GPU parallel-frontier A*; and an experimental evaluation on 14 meshes (up to $7.2$M faces) that ablates the routing cuts and the parallel-frontier search, audits collision freedom and waypoint attainment \emph{after} trajectory smoothing, and measures the end-to-end gap against analytical and LP-relaxation lower bounds. Code and raw results are publicly available.</p></details> |  |
| **[Seeing Together: Multi-Robot Cooperative Egocentric Spatial Reasoning with Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18431v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made substantial progress in egocentric video understanding, but their ability to reason cooperatively from multiple embodied viewpoints remains largely unexplored. We study this problem through multi-robot cooperative dynamic spatial reasoning, where a model must answer spatial, temporal, visibility, and coordination questions by integrating synchronized egocentric videos from a team of moving robots. To support this setting, we introduce CoopSR, the first benchmark for this task, together with EgoTeam, a multi-robot egocentric QA dataset. EgoTeam contains 114,227 QA pairs spanning 19 question types, four difficulty tiers, and three team sizes in Habitat and iGibson, along with a real-world test set of around 2,326 QAs collected using two quadruped robots. SG-CoR combines dynamics-aware multi-robot frame sampling, spectral- and geometric-guided view fusion, and geometry- and motion-informed prompt distillation, enabling the model to benefit from privileged robot-pose supervision during training while requiring only egocentric videos at test time. | **[A Spiking Ring-Attractor Architecture for Representation and Control of Revolute Robot Joints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26027v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic systems with revolute joints evolve on circular state spaces, yet most control architectures represent these states using Euclidean variables that ignore this underlying geometry. We propose a fully spiking neural control architecture in which ring attractors encode joint states, enforcing representations consistent with the topology of revolute systems. Homeostatic regulation computes goal-state mismatch directly over these distributed representations, and opponent gain-modulated populations convert this mismatch into controlled phase shifts of the activity bump, implementing set-point regulation as motion on the ring. We show that the bump dynamics follow an SO(2) geometry, and that gain asymmetries induce a signed phase velocity on the ring. To interface distributed activity with scalar regulation signals, we derive an analytic Fourier phase decoder and implement its signed components using push-pull spiking populations. For multi-joint systems, we demonstrate that local ring-based Fourier features compose into product representations that linearly decode SO(3) orientations through forward kinematics. Experiments in NEST validate the full SO(2) regulation mechanism and the spiking product-feature decoder for lift, pitch, and yaw. Robot experiments on a Tiago arm show that decoded ring dynamics generate joint velocity commands, producing directed motion with joint-dependent accuracy. Together, these results establish a neuromorphic control framework in which representation, regulation, and action are unified within a ring-attractor substrate aligned with the geometry of revolute systems.</p></details> | 23 pages, 10 figures |
| **[SkateNav: Learning Energy-Efficient Navigation through Adaptive Skating on Passive-Wheel Quadrupedal Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25962v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Passive-wheel quadrupedal robots offer energy-efficient locomotion through leg-driven propulsion and momentum-driven gliding. To address these challenges, we propose SkateNav, a unified skating-navigation framework based on reinforcement learning. A time-budgeted goal-reaching objective allows the policy to jointly learn steering, propulsion, and their temporal allocation. An adaptive skating cycle structures gliding, pushing, and recovery, while frequency modulation at every control step adjusts phase durations to navigation demands. Project page: https://ktw1404.github.io/SkateNav/</p></details> |
| **[RoboPocket: Improve Robot Policies Instantly with Your Phone](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05504v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scaling robot learning is bottlenecked not only by data volume, but also by whether the data targets the policy's failures. We present RoboPocket, a smartphone-based system for robot-free policy iteration. By combining on-device sensing, display, and communication with cloud-side policy inference, RoboPocket provides real-time collection guidance and visualizes the policy's predicted trajectory in augmented reality as AR Visual Foresight. This allows users to identify policy weaknesses and collect targeted corrective data without deploying a physical robot. An asynchronous finetuning pipeline continuously incorporates new corrections and updates the policy within minutes, realizing a DAgger-inspired robot-free correction loop. Across diverse real-world manipulation tasks, we validate RoboPocket as a scalable data engine and show that online corrective data collection improves sample efficiency by up to $2\times$ over scaling offline demonstrations. Project page and videos: https://robo-pocket.github.io.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to CoRL 2026. Project page: https://robo-pocket.github.io</p></details> |
| **[Vision-Language-Delta-Action: Learning Compact Deltas as Actionable Transition Interfaces for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25874v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic manipulation is not merely observation-to-action generation, but the realization of task-specified state changes in the physical world. Existing vision-language-action (VLA) methods directly learn observation-to-action mappings, imposing a substantial learning burden due to the gap between semantic understanding and action execution. Although world-action models (WAMs) improve control through future prediction, modeling complete future states wastes capacity on static appearance and action-irrelevant changes. We propose Vision-Language-Delta-Action (VLDA), a paradigm that reformulates full future prediction as compact actionable transition modeling. Its core is a compact Delta variable that captures latent world-state changes, serving as an interface between vision-language understanding and action generation. VLDA learns Delta through a unified objective composed of the Local Transition Filter (LT-Filter) and Global Transition Anchor (GT-Anchor). LT-Filter captures task-relevant manipulation-centric changes while suppressing static reconstruction biases. GT-Anchor provides coarse future-level regularization to preserve the overall direction of state changes. Delta-Driven Action Refinement (DDAR) further grounds Delta in the action manifold and injects it as a structured seed to guide action denoising. We implement VLDA within LoongForge, an open-source training framework for large-scale foundation and embodied models, using its modular and distributed infrastructure for unified two-stage optimization. Experiments show that VLDA improves long-horizon consistency, robustness, and generalization, validating actionable Delta as an alternative to direct action prediction and full future-state modeling.</p></details> | <details><summary>37 pa...</summary><p>37 pages, including appendix. Code framework: https://github.com/baidu-baige/LoongForge/tree/master/cookbook/loongforge-vlda</p></details> |
| **[TrajWAM: An Efficient Trajectory-Guided World Action Model for Dynamic Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25763v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dynamic object manipulation requires robots to anticipate target motion while replanning fast enough to avoid executing outdated actions. We present TrajWAM, a trajectory-guided world action model for efficient dynamic robotic manipulation. Our key insight is to explicitly ground future visual imagination in predicted target-object motion and use the resulting future representation to condition action generation, while preserving this anticipation pathway after acceleration. Given a short history of object masks, a history-conditioned motion predictor forecasts future masks encoding the target object's trajectory. A lightweight Trajectory ControlNet then steers a pretrained video model toward trajectory-consistent futures. The resulting future representation conditions an action expert to predict executable action chunks. To enable one-step inference without breaking this interface, we introduce a decoupled distillation scheme that combines distribution matching for the video backbone, Trajectory ControlNet realignment, and action rollout distillation to support accurate one-step action prediction conditioned on the compressed future representation. Comprehensive evaluations in simulation and the real world show that TrajWAM achieves state-of-the-art performance on dynamic object manipulation tasks while enabling efficient one-step video-action inference. | **[Learning from Fleet Experience: A Collective Learning Paradigm for Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25736v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Experience generated during deployment offers an opportunity for robots to improve policy reliability through learning from their own executions. However, learning through human corrections requires teleoperators to monitor execution and intervene when policies fail, a demand that is difficult to meet as robot fleets grow. We realize CEL through failure-guided experience retrieval fine-tuning (FERT), which uses failure contexts from robot executions to retrieve relevant trajectory segments from the fleet's shared successful experience and uses their actions as supervision for policy updates, without collecting additional expert demonstrations or corrective actions. A fault-tolerant asynchronous training system (FATS) coordinates experience collection, policy optimization and policy distribution despite local interruptions within the robot fleet. Across five real-world manipulation tasks, the resulting policies achieve success rates of 93.3-99.2%, evaluated over 120 trials per task on robots excluded from training data collection. With collection time per robot held fixed, larger fleets provide more experience and FERT generally achieves higher success rates across the evaluated sizes. On multi-stage phone packaging, learning with 96 robots increases success from 79.2% to 98.3%. These results support collective experience as a practical basis for scalable robot learning and a path towards improving policy reliability during deployment without collecting additional expert corrective actions.</p></details> |  |
| **[DeReCo: Decoupling Representation and Coordination Learning for Object-Adaptive Decentralized Multi-Robot Cooperative Grasping and Lifting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08111v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generalizing decentralized multi-robot cooperative grasping and lifting across objects with diverse shapes and physical properties remains a fundamental challenge. Under decentralized execution, two key challenges arise: object-dependent representation learning under partial observability and coordination learning in multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) under non-stationarity. A typical approach jointly optimizes object-dependent representations and coordinated policies in an end-to-end manner while randomizing object shapes and physical properties during training. However, this joint optimization tightly couples representation and coordination learning, which can introduce bidirectional interference: inaccurate representations under partial observability can degrade coordination learning, while non-stationarity in MARL can further degrade representation learning, leading to degraded task performance. Inspired by prior work on decoupling representation and control learning in single-robot settings, we propose DeReCo, a MARL framework that decouples representation and coordination learning for object-adaptive decentralized multi-robot cooperative grasping and lifting. DeReCo adopts a two-phase training strategy: (1) coordination learning with privileged object information, and (2) transition to decentralized execution through reconstruction of object-dependent representations from local observations and retraining of coordination policies using the reconstructed representations. Experimental results show that DeReCo outperforms baselines in simulation on three training objects, generalizes to six unseen objects with varying masses and friction coefficients under similar grasping configurations, and achieves superior performance on two unseen objects in real-robot experiments.</p></details> | 9 pages, 7 figures |

| **[CoEvolve: Self-Evolving Code Policies from Skills to Strategies for Humanoid Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23288v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models and World Action Models (WAMs) have expanded the manipulation and whole body capabilities of individual robots. | **[CusADi2: Latency-Optimized Batch Computation of Robot Kinematics, Jacobians, and Hessians on the GPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25476v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Trajectory optimization methods, from sampling-based zero-order to gradient-based first- and second-order approaches, require many evaluations of dynamical system functions, i.e., functions derived from a robot's dynamical model, such as forward kinematics and their Jacobians and Hessians. Existing frameworks utilize modern GPU hardware primarily to parallelize across batch instances, thereby optimizing throughput but not latency. We present CusADi2, a software library that reduces the batch evaluation latency by additionally parallelizing computation within each batch instance. We benchmark forward kinematics, Jacobians, and Hessians for all 67~robots in the MuJoCo Menagerie, including arms, hands, quadrupeds, humanoids, and biomechanical models, against MuJoCo XLA, MuJoCo Warp, frax, CusADi, and CasADi. CusADi2 achieves lower latency than all baselines at batch sizes of 64 to 4096, while matching them at larger batch sizes. We also demonstrate the performance advantage of CusADi2 in a sampling-based control application. Code: https://github.com/johnviljoen/cusadi2</p></details> |

| **[MVISTA-4D: View-Consistent 4D World Model with Test-Time Action Inference for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09878v3)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World-model-based imagine-then-act becomes a promising paradigm for robotic manipulation, yet existing approaches typically support either purely image-based forecasting or reasoning over partial 3D geometry, limiting their ability to predict complete 4D scene dynamics. | **[EDITH: Teaching Robot to Follow Nonverbal Human Intent from Egocentric Vision and Gaze](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10276v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For natural human-robot interaction, a robot must understand human intent expressed not only through language but also through nonverbal signals such as gestures and gaze. Event cameras offer timely motion observations across strong illumination contrasts, while spiking neural networks provide possible routes to sparse local computation. We examine how sensor degradation, state estimation and computing choices affect feedback and recovery. Higher action throughput need not yield fresher feedback; lower processor power can also coexist with higher energy per inference. We organize validation as an iterative sim-to-ground-to-space pathway, connecting physical tests with telemetry-based diagnosis and closed-loop revalidation. A satellite-capture example links observations to approach, contact and recovery, while a corresponding protocol separates generalization from adaptation with limited target-domain interaction. These comparisons identify what models already contribute and the physical evidence needed to support autonomous space operation.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 7 figures. Critical review. Ancillary files include source inventory and evidence documentation</p></details> |
| **[Robotic Spatiotemporal Editing: A Unified Interface for Controllable Action Generation via Masked Action Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23002v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative policies can synthesize robot behaviors, yet controlling and editing them through diverse spatiotemporal references remains challenging. We introduce the MAP (Masked Action Space) interface to unify these action references and formulate controllable manipulation as an action inpainting problem. Our Inpainting Policy (IP) reconstructs missing action dimensions of the end-effector, combining progress-guided alignment with multi-mask training to support unseen action-constraint combinations. We further adopt a training-free RePaint variant that iteratively inpaints the action during denoising to reduce control error. On ManiSkill and LIBERO-10, our policy achieves average task success rates of 92.67% and 64.67%, respectively.Real-world experiments demonstrate spatiotemporal control with average positional and rotational errors of 44.3 mm and 7°, respectively, showing that our policy can use diverse references to guide executable robot behaviors.</p></details> |  |

| **[Can a Frontier VLM Serve as a Robot Policy for Memory-Dependent Manipulation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17350v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We use a frozen frontier vision-language model, GPT-6-Astra, directly as the robot policy on RoboMME, a benchmark of 16 memory-dependent manipulation tasks spanning temporal, spatial, object, and procedural memory.
